**Reproduce this evaluation.** `bash eval-2026-10-23/run_pipeline5.sh` runs the whole thing: knowledge sweeps, the FRR re-run, the HarmBench ASTRA-D6 re-judgement, the merge, and finally the expensive v1/v2 knowledge sweep. `bash eval-2026-10-23/watchdog2.sh 300` (or `watchdog-loop2.sh`) runs the fail-ops watchdog alongside it. The base model must be present at `<model-cache>/cyberguard-eval/Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507`, the comparator at `.../CyberSecQwen-4B`, and the official judge at `.../HarmBench-Llama-2-13b-cls`; the four adapters are already in the repository at `research-tmp/harvest-v1|harvest-v2|harvest-v3/adapters/`. The analysis layer is separate and re-runnable on its own, from the artifacts, with `<venv>/bin/python`: `safety/hb_paired_all_arms.py` (the paired HarmBench, the replication check, the project's own bars, the trade-off join), `safety/frr_paired_all_arms.py` (the paired FRR), `knowledge_paired_older_arms.py` (the older arms against base and v3 on the knowledge subset), `preflight_merge_610.py` (the merge integration check), `make_results_tables.py` → `splice_section_53.py` (regenerate §5.3 and splice it into this document), and `check_document_consistency.py` + `document_lint.py` (the two cross-document checks). `eval-2026-10-23/README.md` has the method in full and `DIFF-vs-frozen-runner.txt` / `DIFF-safety-runners.txt` prove the instrument was not altered.

**On the orchestration.** Three orchestrator revisions were needed before the pipeline ran cleanly, and each failure is worth recording because they are the same class of failure the project itself kept making — a process that looks alive and is not:

1. A phase-2 orchestrator waited for **both** phase-1 arms before using either GPU; the v3 arm is ~8× slower, so one GPU idled for hours. Fixed by assigning work to whichever GPU is free.
2. Killing an orchestrator left its forked subshells running, and the next launch started a **duplicate** of the same job writing to the same output file — three comparator processes raced for one output path. Fixed with a `flock` single-instance lock.
3. A model-readiness gate that required an index file would have **waited forever** on a legitimate single-file checkpoint. Fixed by accepting weights-with-or-without an index, and by requiring all `.incomplete` blobs to be resolved.
4. Two jobs were scheduled onto the same 24 GiB card, and the collision would have destroyed the **decision-relevant** measurement to protect a diagnostic one. The HarmBench judge dumps its output JSON *inside* its per-model loop, so the file that the ASTRA-D17 diagnostic was waiting on appeared after the **first** arm was judged — while the 13 B judge still held ~21 GiB on GPU0 and ~21 GiB on GPU1. The 4 B diagnostic would then have loaded into a GPU with ~3 GiB free and taken the ASTRA-D6 repair down with it. Caught before it happened, and fixed three ways: the diagnostic's trigger was moved from "the artifact exists" to "the GPU has been released"; it now refuses to start while a judge process is alive or while GPU0 has less than 12 GiB free; and the cells are ordered base-reference-first, so a partially completed diagnostic still yields a valid paired comparison. This is the same class of failure as the three above — **two things that look fine because each is fine in isolation.**
5. The watchdog itself was blind to three of the running jobs. Its discovery regex matched the old `run_phase2.sh` orchestrator but not `run_pipeline5.sh`, and matched neither the ASTRA-D17 waiter nor the ASTRA-D17 worker, so the diagnostic ran entirely unmonitored — the exact "something is running that may have stuck" case the monitor exists for. Fixed in `watchdog2.sh`, which also adds a **GPU-contention check** (two evaluation jobs resident on one card is an alert) and an orchestrator exemption list that covers every `*.sh` waiter, so a flat orchestrator is reported as WAITING and never terminated.
6. **A diagnostic that did not match the ruling that authorised it.** The ASTRA-D17 diagnostic already running covers five cells of 250 MITRE-FRR rows; ASTRA-D17's own text specifies *"128 CTI-MCQ + 128 MITRE-FRR items selected by floor(k*N/128); condition A no system, condition B the declared augmentation-variant prompt; 512 responses"*. The running diagnostic differs on three counts — it has no CTI-MCQ at all, it selects the first 250 rows rather than the specified systematic sample, and it spends 1,250 responses where ASTRA-D17 specified 512 — and the gate's own warning is *"Run it only if it is exactly what that ruling specifies."* The running cells were left alone and a **spec-exact supplement** was added (`run_diag_exact.py`), which imports the sibling diagnostic's decoder unmodified and the frozen runner's own parser, so the decode surface is identical. The lesson is the same one this report keeps finding: **a measurement can be correct, useful, and still not be the thing that was authorised.** It also mattered concretely: CTI-MCQ is where v3's largest public failure lives, and the running diagnostic never touched it.
7. **The harness's own failure mode recurred inside this phase, and it cost 32 minutes of GPU.** The v1 / v2 arm 610-item sweep generated **all three arms** — v1 in 14.3 min, v2 arm A in 5.9 min, v2 arm B in 11.2 min — and then **crashed with `IndexError` in the runner's `--bias` block**, after generation and before the runner's single end-of-run `json.dump`. Every response was lost. The cause is a one-line fragility **inherited from the frozen runner itself**, verified by diff and not introduced by the path-patched copy: the upstream block computes the always-C baseline as `collections.Counter(...).most_common(1)[0]` over the **CTI-MCQ** items, and this sweep selects `--datasets cybermetric-500,secqa-v1-test`, so the Counter is empty and indexing it raises. **The frozen runner was not modified**; only the path-patched copy was made defensive (`if not c:` → *NOT APPLICABLE*), and the diff is recorded in `DIFF-vs-frozen-runner.txt`. Two things are worth recording. First, **this is precisely the project's own B16 failure** — *"the harness still writes only at the end, so a crash loses completed work"* — which this report cites as a cause of the original HarmBench label loss, and it had already been fixed **in this phase's own copy of the safety runners** while the knowledge runner kept the defect. The copy is only as good as the last defect found in it. Second, **the fix is now structural rather than a patch**: responses are persisted **per model the moment each model finishes**, with a `--resume` flag to reuse them, and the sweep was re-run split across both idle GPUs (GPU1: v1 + v2 arm A; GPU0: v2 arm B), with `combine_610_halves.py` joining the halves and recomputing the comparison through the frozen runner's own `summarize()`. **The lesson for the next round is about the copy, not about the bug: when a harness is forked to be path-patched, the fork inherits every latent fragility of the original, and "we fixed it in the safety runner" is not evidence that "it is fixed".** The one honest consolation is that nothing was published from the lost run and no measurement was distorted — the loss was time, not evidence.

The watchdog that catches this class of failure is `eval-2026-10-23/watchdog2.sh`: every 5 minutes it discovers the running jobs from `/proc`, finds each job's real log through its stdout file descriptor, and compares **three independent signals** — the work counter, the process CPU time and the log mtime. A job is declared STUCK only when all three are flat for three consecutive ticks, and it is then terminated so the wait cannot run for days. Orchestrators and wrappers are reported but never killed, because they legitimately sit flat while waiting for a child. Downloads are watched by bytes, and a download whose process has exited with no unresolved blobs is reported COMPLETE rather than STUCK. It also lists the compute apps per GPU and raises an alert when two or more evaluation jobs are resident on the same card — the failure that lesson 4 above nearly produced.
